Higher derivatives of the end-point map of a control-linear system via adapted coordinates Keywords : control-linear system, end-point map, variation, jets, Taylor expansion, special coordinates, sub-Riemannian geodesic MSC 2020 : 93C10, 93B11, 93C73, 53C17
Michał Jóźwikowski, [1ex] Bartłomiej Sikorski, [2ex] Faculty of Mathematics, Informatics, Mechanics[1ex] University of Warsaw, [2ex] Department of Mathematics[1ex] University of Fribourg

Abstract
We study the end-point map of a control-linear system in a neighborhood of an arbitrarily chosen trajectory. In particular, we want to calculate the k k -th order derivative of this map in a given direction. A priori it is a solution of a quite complicated ODE depending on all derivatives of order less or equal k k . We prove that there exists a special coordinate system adapted to the geometry of the problem, which changes the system of ODEs describing all derivatives of the end-point map up to order k k to equations of a control-affine (non-autonomous control-linear) system, with the direction of derivation playing the role of the new control. As an application we study controllability criteria for this system, obtaining first and second-order necessary optimality conditions of sub-Riemannian geodesics. In particular, for the case of an abnormal minimizer we can interpret Goh conditions as non-controllability conditions of this control-affine system for k = 2 k=2 . We make a hypothesis that for higher k k ’s its non-controllability corresponds to recently obtained higher-order analogs of the Goh conditions [Boarotto, Monti, Palmurella, 2020], [Boarotto, Monti, a Socionovo, 2022].



1
Introduction

The problem

Local properties of the end-point map around a trajectory can be quite significant in some aspects of control theory. They play an important role in phenomena such as local controllability, abnormality, and optimality of trajectories. Let us name a few examples. For optimal control problems openness of the (extended, i.e. including the costs) end-point map around a trajectory excludes the possibility that this trajectory is optimal. Therefore non-openness of the end-point map gives necessary conditions for optimality. By using the standard version of the Open Mapping Theorem (
OMT
) we get first-order conditions for optimality, i.e equations of the Pontryagin Maximum Principle. These conditions are also fundamental for classifying extremals of the optimal control problem as
normal
or
abnormal

[
Jur97
,
AS04
]
. In some specific situations, like the study of abnormal sub-Riemannian geodesics, the above conditions are, however, insufficient. Therefore one needs to use more specific versions of the OMT. A degree-two version is a basis of
[
AS96
,
Jóź23
]
, and recently there has been an attempt of using Sussmann’s version of the OMT
[
Sus03
]
to study sub-Riemannian optimality conditions of any order
[
BMP20
,
BMS22
]
.

For a control system on a manifold
M
, whose set of admissible controls
\Omega
forms a vector space, perhaps the most natural idea to study the time
t
-end-point map
\mathrm{End}^{t}:\Omega\rightarrow M
is by calculating its Taylor expansion. In this paper we consider such an expansion for a control-linear system

\dot{q}(t)=\sum_{i}^{l}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(q(t))\ ,

(
\Lambda
)

where
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{l})\in\Omega\subset\operatorname{Meas}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
is the control, and
X_{i}
, for
i=1,2,\ldots,l
are linearly independent vector fields on
M
.
Our goal is to calculate the
k
-th order derivative of the end-point map at a given control
u\in\Omega
in the direction of a control
\Delta u\in\mathrm{T}_{u}\Omega\simeq\Omega
, i.e
\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]=\frac{\operatorname{d}^{k}}{\operatorname{d}s^{k}}\big|_{s=0}\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\cdot\Delta u]
. At this point, it is worth to remark, that as
\mathrm{End}^{t}
is a manifold-valued map, such a derivative usually has no geometric sense, although it can be calculated in any chosen local coordinate system. This issue can be easily resolved by using the geometric notion of a
k
-jet, rather than a coordinate-dependent notion of a
k
-th derivative. We comment on this matter in Remark
1.1
.

Our results

First of all, in Lemma
2.1
, we were able to derive a hierarchy of ODEs, linear in both
u
and
\Delta u
, describing the desired derivatives in a local coordinate system. Our main result is, however, an observation that there exists a time-dependent family of diffeomorphisms (depending on the given control
u
and the vector fields
X_{i}
) which transforms the differential equations describing the set of derivatives

(\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u],\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u],\ldots,\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u])\ ,

i.e. the
k
-jet of the curve
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}(u+s\cdot\Delta u)
, into a system of ODEs for new variables
(q^{(1)},q^{(2)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
, which is linear in
\Delta u
, and has no explicit dependence of the initial control
u
.
These transformations are described by a family of multi-linear maps – their construction and properties are stated as Theorem
3.2
. For reasons that will be clarified in the next paragraph, we call these newly constructed variables –
adapted coordinates
. As a corollary, we formulate Theorem
3.5
which states that
(q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
– the solutions of the considered ODE system in adapted coordinates – are trajectories of a non-autonomous control-linear system (or, as we prefer to see it, a control-affine system in variables
(t,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
with the drift being simply
\partial_{t}
) with
\Delta u
playing the role of a linear control. Moreover, this system of ODEs is naturally graded. The above results, i.e. Lemma
2.1
, Theorem
3.2
, and Theorem
3.5
, are further generalized as, respectively, Lemma
5.1
, Theorem
5.2
and Theorem
5.3
to describe
k
-jets of curves
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}(u+s\cdot\Delta u^{(1)}+s^{2}\cdot\Delta u^{(2)}+\ldots+s^{k}\cdot\Delta u^{(k)})
, which seem to be more promising from the point of view of applications.

The idea behind adapted coordinates is actually quite easy to explain. Note that after fixing a control
u
, system (

Λ

) evolves according to a time-dependent vector field
X_{u}(t,q)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(q)
. Under mild assumption this evolution produces a time-dependent flow
X^{tt^{\prime}}_{u}
acting on
M
(being a groupoid rather than a group). Now the tangent map of this flow acts on
\mathrm{T}M
and, in particular, it gives a distinguished evolution in the tangent spaces
\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M
along a given trajectory of the system
q(t)
. Thus after fixing a basis
\{e_{1}(0),\ldots,e_{n}(0)\}
of
\mathrm{T}_{q(0)}M
, the flow spreads it into a family of bases
\{e_{1}(t),\ldots,e_{n}(t)\}
, each for every particular tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M
. Adapted coordinates
q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)
are precisely the coordinates of the tangent vector
\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]
in this new basis. The reason why the evolution equation for
t\mapsto\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]
looks simpler in these new coordinates is because the natural evolution by the flow of the control vector field
X_{u}(t,q)
was included in their construction. In other words, the new coordinates are
adapted
to the chosen evolution of the system.

Higher-order adapted coordinates
(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
are constructed analogously, by considering the action of the flow of the control vector field on
k
-jets. Thus one should think that
(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
are coordinates in
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M
– the space of
k
-jets of curves passing through the base point
q(t)
– naturally ,,preferred” by the evolution on
M
given by the field
X_{u}(t,q)
. We clarify this intuition in Lemma
3.4
. The presence of a natural graded structure on
\mathrm{T}^{k}M

[
Sau89
,
GR12
]
is the reason why we observe the presence of the natural grading in the ODE systems in Theorems
3.2
and
3.5
. The theory is illustrated with a study of a particular system of a generalized Martinet system, running throughout the text (Examples
2.2
,
3.3
and
3.6
).

We propose two particular applications of the presented theory. First, in Subsection
4.1
, we calculate the adapted coordinates for an invariant control system on a matrix group. In this case the construction turns out to be quite simple. Moreover, it allows for an easy derivation of some formulas of Le Donne
[
LD21
]
. We also comment on the case of a general Lie group.

Later in Subsection
4.2
, we study second-order optimality conditions for a sub-Riemannian geodesic problem. Our idea is to interpret the assertion of Agrachev-Sarychev Index Lemma
[
AS96
]
as non-controllability conditions for a certain control system involving first and second derivatives of the end-point map. However, by Theorem
3.5
, in adapted coordinates
(t,q^{(1)},q^{(2)})
this system is a control-affine system, and we may address the question of its controllability using the results of Sussmann and Jurdjevic
[
SJ72
]
. It turns out that the criteria for non-controllability give precisely the
Goh conditions

[
Goh66
]
(see Lemmas
4.6
and
4.7
). It seems to us that such an interpretation of Goh conditions was not present in the literature so far, not to mention that (once the Agrachev-Sarychev Index Lemma is known) the proof does not require making any estimates. This observation is a basis of a Hypothesis
5.4
(backup-ed by some calculations) that higher-order Goh conditions introduced recently in
[
BMP20
,
BMS22
]
are a consequence of non-controllability of a control-affine system described by Theorem
5.3
.
We also refer to a publication of one of us
[
Jóź23
]
for further applications of adapted coordinates in sub-Riemannian geometry. Here these were used to study the geometry of second-order approximation of the end-point map around a minimizing trajectory.

A remark about jets

Remark 1.1
.

In general, there is a sense to speak about higher derivatives only for maps valued in a vector space. Indeed, consider the end-point map
\mathrm{End}^{t}:\Omega\longrightarrow M
at
u\in\Omega
. Let
\phi:M\supset U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be local coordinates on
M
around
p=\mathrm{End}^{t}(u)
. We may then calculate the Taylor expansion of
\phi\circ\mathrm{End}^{t}:\Omega\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, but then, say, the
k
-th order term will not transform well when passing to a new coordinate system. In other words,
\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]
is not a well-defined geometric object for
k\geq 2
.

There are essentially two ways to deal with this problem. The first one is to restrict our attention to a situation in which a higher derivative makes sense. This strategy has been used in some Agrachev’s works – see for example
[
AS04
]
, where the second derivative
\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}
is defined on
\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}
and takes values in
\operatorname{coker}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}=\mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{End}^{t}(u)}M/\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}
. In a moment we shall explain why it is so. Similar constructions are present, for instance, in
[
BMP20
,
BMS22
]
.

In this paper we prefer a different approach. Instead of being interested in each particular term of the Taylor expansion, we want to consider the whole series up to a term of a given order
k
. This can be formalized in the language of jets as follows (cf.
[
Sau89
]
). We say that two curves
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\rightarrow M
passing through
p=\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)\in M
are
tangent up to order
k
at
p
, if in some (and thus any) coordinate system
\phi:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
around
p
we have for all
m=1,2\ldots,k
:

\frac{\operatorname{d}^{m}}{\operatorname{d}s^{m}}\Big|_{s=0}\phi(\gamma(s))=\frac{\operatorname{d}^{m}}{\operatorname{d}s^{m}}\Big|_{s=0}\phi(\gamma^{\prime}(s))\ .

The relation of the
k
-th order tangency is an equivalence relation and its equivalence classes are called
k
-jets
. We will denote the space of all
k
-jets on
M
by
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
. The assignment of a
k
-jet to its base point
[\gamma]_{\sim_{k}}\mapsto p=\gamma(0)
makes
\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow M
a locally trivial bundle. In fact, this is an example of a
graded bundle
in the sense of Grabowski and Rotkiewicz
[
GR12
]
, meaning that the fibers
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{p}M
are endowed with a canonical action of the multiplicative reals. Actually, the structure of
\mathrm{T}^{k}M
is much more specific, namely natural projections
[\gamma]_{\sim i}\mapsto[\gamma]_{\sim(i-1)}
give rise to the tower of fibrations
\mathrm{T}^{k}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k-1}M\rightarrow\ldots\mathrm{T}^{2}M\rightarrow\mathrm{T}M\rightarrow M
, where the first level is a vector bundle and each higher level is an affine bundle modeled on
\mathrm{T}M
(see
[
Sau89
]
).

More specifically, in this paper we will consider the Taylor expansion of the end-point map, which after identifying a manifold
M
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by a choice of local coordinates may be written as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\Delta u]\overset{loc}{=}\mathrm{End}^{t}[u]+s\cdot\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]+s^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2!}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]+\ldots+s^{k}\cdot\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{D}^{k}\mathrm{End}^{t}_{u}[\Delta u]+o(s^{k})\ .

As we mentioned earlier, individual terms
\mathrm{D}^{i}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]
have no geometric meaning for
i\geq 2
, yet their whole collection
(\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u],\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u],\ldots,\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u])
, encoding the
k
-jet of a curve
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\Delta u]
, does. This can be to some extent seen at the level of ODEs (
2.2
) which describe the time evolution of this
k
-jet: equation for
\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]
contains terms depending on lower-order derivatives, hence there is no way to separate
\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}[\Delta u]
from the rest of the collection
(\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u],\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u],\ldots,\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u])
.

Finally, note that the concept of a jet allows to explain the understanding of second (and higher – see
[
BMP20
]
) derivatives in the spirit of Argachev. Namely, take a curve
u_{s}=u+s\cdot u^{(1)}+s^{2}\cdot u^{(2)}+o(s^{2})
in
\Omega
, and let us calculate the second Taylor expansion of
\mathrm{End}(u_{s})
in some local coordinate system:

\mathrm{End}(u+s\cdot u^{(1)}+s^{2}\cdot u^{(2)})\overset{loc}{=}\mathrm{End}(u)+s\cdot\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(1)}\right]+s^{2}\cdot\left(\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(2)}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(1)},u^{(1)}\right]\right)+o(s^{2})\ .

(1.1)

Thus to extract the
\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(1)},u^{(1)}\right]
-term from the whole 2-jet
(\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(1)}\right],\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(2)}\right]+\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(1)},u^{(1)}\right])
one needs first to assume that
\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(1)}\right]=0
and then quotient out the
\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}\left[u^{(2)}\right]
-term, precisely as in the Agrachev’s approach (where the second derivative is defined for
u^{(1)}\in\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}
, and takes values in
\operatorname{coker}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}
).

Notation: graded multi-indexes and the polynomial expansion of a composition of maps

Remark 1.2

(Notation convention)
.

Consider a multi-index
\alpha=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k})
and let us introduce the following notation:

|\alpha|:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}
is the
absolute value
of
\alpha
,

w(\alpha):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\cdot a_{k}
is the
weight
of
\alpha

\displaystyle\alpha!:=a_{1}!\cdot a_{2}!\cdot\ldots\cdot a_{k}!\ .

It is convenient to think that
\alpha
is graded, with
a_{i}
being of weight
i
. Moreover, for an
|\alpha|
-linear map
\Phi^{|\alpha|}
and
\vec{b}=(b^{(1)},b^{(2)},\ldots,b^{(k)})
we define

\Phi^{|\alpha|}\left[\vec{b}^{\ \alpha}\right]:=\Phi^{|\alpha|}\Big[\underbrace{b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(1)}}_{a_{1}},\underbrace{b^{(2)},\ldots,b^{(2)}}_{a_{2}},\ldots,\underbrace{b^{(k)},\ldots,b^{(k)}}_{a_{k}}\Big]\ .

Throughout this paper we understand gradings and weights according to
[
GR12
]
.

The above convention allows for an elegant description of the polynomial expansion of a composition of a function and a curve.

Lemma 1.3

(Faa di Bruno)
.

Consider a smooth curve
\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, whose polynomial expansion at
s=0
reads as

\gamma(s)=\gamma(0)+s\cdot\gamma^{(1)}+s^{2}\cdot\gamma^{(2)}+\ldots+s^{k}\cdot\gamma^{(k)}+o(s^{k})\ ,

and let
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function. Then their composition
F:=f(\gamma):(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
expands as

F(s)=F(0)+s\cdot F^{(1)}+s^{2}\cdot F^{(2)}+\ldots s^{k}\cdot F^{(k)}+o(s^{k})\ ,

where

F^{(m)}=\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\alpha|}_{\gamma(0)}f\left[\vec{\gamma}^{\ \alpha}\right]\

(1.2)

with
\vec{\gamma}=(\gamma^{(1)},\gamma^{(2)},\ldots,\gamma^{(k)})
.

We leave the proof as an exercise.

2
Higher derivatives of the end-point map

A control-linear system

Consider a control-linear system (

Λ

) on an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
given by a rank-
l
distribution
\mathcal{D}_{p}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{X_{1}(p),\ldots,X_{l}(p)\}\subset\mathrm{T}_{p}M
, for all
p\in M
:

\dot{q}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(q(t));\quad\text{with the initial condition $q(0)=q_{0}$,}

(
\Lambda
)

where
u(t)=(u_{1}(t),\ldots,u_{l}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
is the control. We assume that
u
belongs to some space
\Omega
. Unless specified differently, we will assume only that
\Omega
is a vector subspace of the space of measurable maps
\operatorname{Meas}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
.

The end-point map

For each
t\in[0,T]
denote by

\mathrm{End}^{t}:\Omega\longrightarrow M;\qquad\mathrm{End}^{t}:u\longmapsto q(t)\text{ satisfying \eqref{eqn:lin_ctrl_syst}}\

the family of the
end-point maps
related with (

Λ

). Our goal is to calculate
\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]
– the
k
-th derivative of
\mathrm{End}^{t}
at a given control
u
in the direction of
\Delta u\in\mathrm{T}_{u}\Omega\simeq\Omega
, for each
k=1,2,3,\ldots
.

To do this let us consider a family of solutions
q_{s}(t)
of (

Λ

) corresponding to the controls
u+s\cdot\Delta u
, i.e.

\dot{q}_{s}(t)=\sum_{i}(u_{i}(t)+s\cdot\Delta u_{i}(t))X_{i}(q_{s}(t));\quad\text{with $q_{s}(0)=q_{0}$.}

(2.1)

The above is an ODE in the sense of Caratheodory smoothly depending on the parameter
s
. As such it has a solution smoothly depending on
s
– see
[
BP04
]
. The Taylor expansion of
s\mapsto q_{s}(t)
at
s=0
(is some local coordinate system) gives us

\displaystyle q_{s}(t)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\Delta u]\overset{loc}{=}

\displaystyle\mathrm{End}^{t}[u]+s\cdot\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]+s^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2!}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]+\ldots+s^{k}\cdot\frac{1}{k!}\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]+o(s^{k})=

\displaystyle q(t)+s\cdot b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)+s^{2}\cdot b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)+\ldots s^{k}\cdot b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)+o(s^{k})\ ,

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u):=\frac{1}{m!}\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial s^{m}}\Big|_{s=0}q_{s}(t)=\frac{1}{m!}\mathrm{D}^{m}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u].

Actually, in light of Remark
1.1
, there is a sense to speak about higher derivatives
\mathrm{D}^{k}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{t}[\Delta u]
only if we locally identify
M
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by means of a particular choice of local coordinates. Therefore, while reading the remaining part of this article the Reader should remember that the ODEs characterizing curves
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
are derived in a particular coordinate frame. However, in spite of that, the whole
k
-jet of the curve
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\cdot\Delta u]
at
s=0
, i.e.

q(t)+s\cdot b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)+s^{2}\cdot b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)+\ldots s^{k}\cdot b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)+o(s^{k})

is a well-defined geometric notion. We shall refer to it as a
k
-variation
of
\mathrm{End}^{t}
at
u
in the direction of
\Delta u
.

As
\dot{b}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=\frac{1}{m!}\partial^{m}_{s}\big|_{s=0}\dot{q}_{s}(t)
, for each
m=1,2,\ldots,k
curves
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
are solutions of a system of ODEs obtained by an
m
-fold differentiation of (
2.1
) at
s=0
. The first few of these equations are easy to calculate:

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}u_{i}\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(1)}]+\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}X_{i}\Big|_{q(t)}

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}u_{i}\left(\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(2)}]+\frac{1}{2!}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}]\right)+\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(1)}]

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(3)}(t,\Delta u)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}u_{i}\left(\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(3)}]+\mathrm{D}^{2}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(2)}]+\frac{1}{3!}\mathrm{D}^{3}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(1)},b^{(1)}]\right)+

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\left(\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(2)}]+\frac{1}{2!}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}]\right)\ .

Here
q(t)=q_{0}(t)
is a trajectory of (

Λ

) corresponding to the control
u
. A clear inductive pattern begins to be seen. Moreover, the above equations are naturally graded after putting
\deg(b^{(m)})=m
and
\deg(\Delta u_{i})=1
. In general, we have the following result

Lemma 2.1

(the general form of
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
s)
.

Consider the
k
-jet of the curve
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\cdot\Delta u]
at
s=0
given in local coordinates by

\mathrm{End}^{t}(u+s\cdot\Delta u)\overset{loc}{=}q(t)+s\cdot b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)+s^{2}\cdot b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)+\ldots s^{k}\cdot b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)+o(s^{k})\ .

Then curves
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)

are subject to the following system of ODEs:

\begin{split}\dot{b}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=&\sum_{i}u_{i}\left(\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\alpha|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha}]\right)+\\
&\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\left(\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=m-1}\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\beta}]\right)\end{split}

(2.2)

where we use the notation introduced on page
1.2
, i.e. the summation is taken over all multi-indices
\alpha=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots)
of a given weight
w(\alpha):=\sum_{i}i\cdot a_{i}
equal
m
and
m-1
, respectively,
\mathrm{D}^{|\alpha|}_{q(t)}X_{i}
denotes the
|\alpha|
-th derivative of
X_{i}
at
q(t)
understood as a
|\alpha|
-linear map, and
\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)=(b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
.

Proof.

The
m
-fold differentiation of (
2.1
) gives us

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\left(\partial_{s}^{m}q_{s}(t)\right)=

\displaystyle\partial_{s}^{m}\left(\dot{q_{s}}(t)\right)=\partial_{s}^{m}\left[\sum_{i}\left(u_{i}(t)+s\cdot\Delta u_{i}(t)\right)X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\partial_{s}^{m}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]+\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\partial_{s}^{m}\left[s\cdot X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\partial_{s}^{m}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]+\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\left\{m\cdot\partial_{s}^{m-1}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]+s\cdot\partial_{s}^{m}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]\right\}

Now considering the above equality at
s=0
, since
\partial_{s}^{m}\big|_{0}q_{s}(t)=m!\cdot b^{(m)}(t)
we get

\displaystyle m!\cdot\dot{b}^{(m)}(t)=\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\partial_{s}^{m}\Big|_{0}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]+m\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\partial_{s}^{m-1}\Big|_{0}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]

Finally
q_{s}(t)
expands as

q_{s}(t)=q(t)+s\cdot b^{(1)}(t)+s^{2}\cdot b^{(2)}(t)+\ldots s^{k}\cdot b^{(k)}(t)+o(s^{k})

so, by the results of Lemma
1.3
,

\displaystyle\partial_{s}^{m}\Big|_{0}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]=m!\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\alpha|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha}]\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\partial_{s}^{m-1}\Big|_{0}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]=(m-1)!\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=m-1}\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}]

Thus (
2.2
) holds.
∎

Let us comment briefly on the question of the existence of solutions of the system (
2.2
). On the one hand, we may argue that by a general result of existence, uniqueness, and parameter-regularity of ODEs in the sense of Caratheodory – see for example
[
BP04
]
– curves
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
should be well defined for all
t
for which
q(t)
satisfying (

Λ

) makes sense. We may, however, see this fact from a more technical perspective. Note namely, that if the
(m-1)
-jet
(b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,b^{(m-1)}(t,\Delta u))
is known, then by (
2.2
), the equation for
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
takes the form of an affine ODE

\dot{b}^{(m)}=\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(m)}]+f(t)\ ,

where
f(t)
is measurable. Thus, whenever one finds a fundamental solution of the homogeneous part of this equation,

\dot{b}=\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b]\ ,

b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
can be derived by the standard Cauchy formula. Yet the fundamental solution of the homogeneous part is simply the tangent map of the time-dependent flow of the control vector field
X_{u}(t,q)=\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(q)
. Thus it is indeed well-defined whenever the flow of
X_{u}(t,q)
is well-defined. For a more detailed discussion of time-dependent flows and their tangent maps see
[
JR16
, Sec. 2]
.

Example 2.2

(Generalized Martinet system, part 1.)
.

After
[
BMP20
]
, consider a control system in
\mathbb{R}^{3}\ni(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
given by a pair of vector fields

X_{1}=\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{1}}}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{2}=(1-x_{1})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}+x_{1}^{p}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{3}}\ ,

where
p>1
is an integer.
Let
u(t)\equiv(0,1)
be the control corresponding to a trajectory
q(t)=(0,t,0)
, and take another control
\Delta u(t)=(\Delta u_{1}(t),\Delta u_{2}(t))
. Let us describe curves
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
for
m=1,2,\ldots,p,p+1
in this setting.

We have
\mathrm{D}^{m}_{q(t)}X_{1}\equiv 0
for every
m=1,2,\ldots
, while only non-trivial derivatives of
X_{2}
along the considered trajectory are

\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{2}[\cdot]=-\big\langle\operatorname{d}x_{1},\cdot\big\rangle\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{D}^{p}_{q(t)}X_{2}[\underbrace{\cdot,\cdot,\ldots,\cdot}_{p}]=p!\big\langle\operatorname{d}x_{1},\cdot\big\rangle^{p}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}\ .

It follows that equations (
2.2
) look as follows

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(1)}(t)

\displaystyle=-b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}+\Delta u_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{1}}+\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle=-b^{(2)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(m)}(t)

\displaystyle=-b^{(m)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(m-1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(p)}(t)

\displaystyle=-b^{(p)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(p-1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}+{\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}}

\displaystyle\dot{b}^{(p+1)}(t)

\displaystyle=-b^{(p+1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(p)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}+p\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p-1}b^{(2)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}+\Delta u_{2}(t)\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}\ .

As we see the first appearance of the
\partial_{x_{3}}
direction happens in degree
p
.

Since the initial values are
b^{(m)}_{i}(0)=0
, for every
m=1,2,\ldots,p+1
and
i=1,2,3
, it is easy to solve the above system:

\displaystyle b^{(1)}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{1}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau

\displaystyle b^{(1)}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau-\int_{0}^{t}b^{(1)}_{1}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau-\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\Delta u_{1}(s)\operatorname{d}s\right)\operatorname{d}\tau

\displaystyle b^{(2)}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\cdot b^{(1)}_{1}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau=-\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\Delta u_{1}(s)\operatorname{d}s\right)\operatorname{d}\tau

\displaystyle b^{(p)}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(\tau)\right)^{p}\operatorname{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\Delta u_{1}(s)\operatorname{d}s\right)^{p}\operatorname{d}\tau

\displaystyle b^{(p+1)}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\cdot\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(\tau)\right)^{p}\operatorname{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\Delta u_{1}(s)\operatorname{d}s\right)^{p}\operatorname{d}\tau\ ,

with all other components
b^{(m)}_{i}(t)
, for
m=1,2,\ldots,p+1
, equal to zero.

3
Addapted coordinates

The idea of adapted coordinates

Recall the expansion

\mathrm{End}^{t}(u+s\cdot\Delta u)\overset{loc}{=}q(t)+s\cdot b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)+{s^{2}}\cdot b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)+{s^{3}}\cdot b^{(3)}(t,\Delta u)+\ldots

(with
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
’s as in Lemma
2.1
) which describes the derivatives of the end-point maps
\mathrm{End}^{t}
at
u
in the direction
\Delta u
. As we have seen, the formulas for
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
’s are quite complicated. Our goal is to introduce, for each
m=1,2,\ldots
, a family of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued maps
q^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=\left(q^{(m)}_{a}(t,\Delta u)\right)_{a=1,2,\ldots,n}
, which satisfy the following two properties:

(i)

there is 1-1 correspondence between
(b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
and
\left(q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)\right)
for each natural
k
;

(ii)

the derivatives
{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
are linear in
\Delta u
.

It is therefore justified to treat
\left(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\cdot k}
as coordinates on the space of
k
-jets
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M\ni(b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})
’s. We will call them
adapted coordinates
, meaning that they are specially adapted to the geometry of the control system (

Λ

) along the trajectory
q(t)
.

The idea is to define
q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)
’s comes from Lemma
1.3
– we apply formulas (
1.2
) describing the expansion of a composition of a function and a curve to
s\mapsto q_{s}(t)
, with
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
’s playing the roles of
\gamma^{(m)}
’s and some, a priori unknown,
m
-linear symmetric forms
\Phi^{(m)}_{a}(t):\underbrace{\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M\times\ldots\times\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M}_{m}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
playing the roles of the derivatives
\mathrm{D}^{m}_{\gamma(0)}f
’s. The evolution of these
\Phi^{(m)}_{a}(t)
will be determined later to guarantee that the evolution of all
q^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
’s satisfies condition
(
ii
)
. The geometric meaning of this construction will be discussed in Lemma
3.4
.

Adapted coordinates in degrees 1, 2, and 3

Let us study a few examples of a low degree before passing to the general case. For each
a=1,2\ldots,n
define:

\displaystyle q^{(1)}_{a}(t,\Delta u):=

\displaystyle\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)\left[b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)\right]

(3.1)

\displaystyle q^{(2)}_{a}(t,\Delta u):=

\displaystyle\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)\left[b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(2)}_{a}(t)\left[b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)\right]

(3.2)

\displaystyle q^{(3)}_{a}(t,\Delta u):=

\displaystyle\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)[b^{(3)}(t,\Delta u)]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}(t)\left[b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u),b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)\right]+

(3.3)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3!}\Phi^{(3)}_{a}(t)\left[b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)\right]\ ,

We postulate
\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(0)\ |\ a=1,2,\ldots,n\}
to be a fixed basis of
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(0)}M
, and set
\Phi^{(k)}_{a}(0)=0
for
k=2
,
k=3
, and each
a=1,2,\ldots,n
. Now the evolution of
q^{(1)}_{a}(t)
reads as

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(1)}_{a}(t,\Delta u)=

\displaystyle{\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}\left[b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\dot{b}^{(1)}\right]=\underbrace{{\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}\left[b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\sum_{i}u_{i}\mathrm{D}X_{i}\left[b^{(1)}\right]\right]}_{\text{assume $=0$ to satisfy \eqref{point:ii}}}+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}X_{i}\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\Phi^{(1)}_{a}[X_{i}]\ .

Therefore we postulate the following evolution of
\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)
:

{\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}(t)[b]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b]\right]=0\quad\text{ for every $b\in\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M$.}

(3.4)

Clearly, the above evolution is dual to the evolution on
\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M
by the flow of the time-dependent vector field
q\longmapsto\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(q)
. This guarantees that for each
t\in[0,T]
covectors
\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)
form a basis of
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(t)}M
. In consequence, the procedure of constructing maps
q^{(k)}(t)
from
b^{(k)}(t)
’s will be reversible for each
k=1,2,\ldots
.

In degree two we have:

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(2)}_{a}(t,\Delta u)=

\displaystyle{\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}\left[b^{(2)}\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\dot{b}^{(2)}\right]+\frac{1}{2!}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(2)}_{a}\left[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\dot{b}^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]=

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}\left[b^{(2)}\right]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}\left[b^{(2)}\right]\right]}_{=0\text{ by \eqref{eqn:ew_Phi_1}}}+

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{2!}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(2)}_{a}\left[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}\left[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}\left[b^{(1)}\right],b^{(1)}\right]\right\}}_{\text{assume $=0$ to satisfy \eqref{point:ii}}}+

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\left\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(1)}]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[X_{i},b^{(1)}\right]\right\}\ .

Therefore we postulate

{\dot{\Phi}}^{(2)}_{a}[b,b]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{2\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b],b\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[b,b]\right]\right\}=0\qquad\text{ for every $b\in\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M$.}

If the above holds then, since
\Phi^{(2)}_{a}
is symmetric 2-linear, for every
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M
we have:

{\dot{\Phi}}^{(2)}_{a}[b,b^{\prime}]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[b,b^{\prime}]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b],b^{\prime}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{\prime}],b\right]\right\}=0\ .

(3.5)

Analogously in degree 3:

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(3)}_{a}(t)

\displaystyle={\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}\left[b^{(3)}\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\dot{b}^{(3)}\right]+{\dot{\Phi}}^{(2)}_{a}\left[b^{(2)},b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\dot{b}^{(2)},b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[b^{(2)},\dot{b}^{(1)}\right]+\frac{1}{3!}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(3)}_{a}\left[b^{(1)},b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(3)}_{a}\left[\dot{b}^{(1)},b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]=

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}\left[b^{(3)}\right]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(3)}]\right]}_{=0\text{ by \eqref{eqn:ew_Phi_1}}}+

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\dot{\Phi}}^{(2)}_{a}\left[b^{(2)},b^{(1)}\right]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[b^{(2)},b^{(1)}]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(2)}],b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(1)}],b^{(2)}\right]\right\}}_{\text{$=0$ by \eqref{eqn:ew_Phi_2}}}+

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{3!}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(3)}_{a}\left[b^{(1)},b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\left\{\frac{1}{3!}\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{3}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(1)},b^{(1)}]\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}],b^{(1)}\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(3)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(1)}],b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]\right\}}_{\text{assume $=0$ to satisfy \eqref{point:ii}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\left\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(2)}]\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(1)}],b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[X_{i},b^{(2)}\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(3)}_{a}\left[X_{i},b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]\right\}\ .

Therefore we postulate that for every
b\in\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M

{\dot{\Phi}}^{(3)}_{a}[b,b,b]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{3\Phi^{(3)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b],b,b\right]+3\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[b,b],b\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{3}X_{i}[b,b,b]\right]\right\}=0

(3.6)

Summing up, under assumptions (
3.4
)–(
3.6
), our new coordinates (
3.1
)–(
3.3
), evolve as follows:

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(1)}_{a}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[X_{i}\right]

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(2)}_{a}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\left\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(1)}]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[X_{i},b^{(1)}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(3)}_{a}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\left\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(2)}]\right]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[b^{(1)}],b^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}_{a}\left[X_{i},b^{(2)}\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(3)}_{a}\left[X_{i},b^{(1)},b^{(1)}\right]\right\}

The general case

The pattern observed for terms up to order 3, continues in all degrees. We may thus generalize the transformations (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) (satisfying conditions (
3.4
)–(
3.6
)) in the following definition.

Definition 3.1
.

Consider a trajectory
q(t)
of a control-linear system (

Λ

) corresponding to the control
u\in\Omega
. Choose a local coordinate system on
M
and for each
m=1,2,\ldots
define a time-dependent family of symmetric
m
-linear maps

\Phi^{(m)}(t)=\left(\Phi^{(m)}_{a}(t)\right)_{a=1,2,\ldots,n}:\underbrace{\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M\times\ldots\times\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M}_{\text{$m$ times}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}

by setting the following conditions:

•

we set
\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(0)\}_{a=1,2\ldots,n}
to be a basis of
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(0)}M
, while for
m=2,3,\ldots
maps
\Phi^{(m)}(0)
can be arbitrary,

•

\Phi^{(m)}(t)
’s are subject to the following ODEs:

{\dot{\Phi}}^{(m)}(t)[\underbrace{b,....,b}_{\text{$m$ times}}]+\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\cdot\left\{\sum_{s=1}^{m}{m\choose s}\Phi^{(m-s+1)}(t)\left[\mathrm{D}^{s}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{\text{$s$ times}}],\underbrace{b,\ldots,b}_{\text{$m-s$ times}}\right]\right\}=0\ .

(3.7)

For each
k=1,2,\ldots
and
t\in[0,T]
the assignment

\Psi^{(k)}(t):\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M\ni(b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})\longmapsto(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\cdot k}\ ,

given by (within the notation convention of Rem.
1.2
)

q^{(m)}=\left(q^{(m)}_{a}\right)_{a=1,2,\ldots,n}:=\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}(t)\left[\vec{b}^{\alpha}\right]\

(3.8)

will be called the
transformation of adapted coordinates
of degree
k
at time
t
.

We summarize basic properties of the above notion in the following result.

Theorem 3.2
.

For every
t\in[0,T]
, and every
k
, the transformation of adapted coordinates
\Psi^{(k)}(t)
does not depend on the choice of a local coordinate system. Further it is an isomorphism between the fibre
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M\ni(b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})
and
\mathbb{R}^{n\cdot k}\ni(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
respecting the natural graded structures in which
\deg(b^{(i)})=\deg(q^{(i)})=i
.

For any control
\Delta u\in\mathrm{T}_{u}\Omega\simeq\Omega
consider the
k
-jet of the curve
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\cdot\Delta u]
at
s=0
given in local coordinates by

\mathrm{End}^{t}(u+s\cdot\Delta u)\overset{loc}{=}q(t)+s\cdot b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)+s^{2}\cdot b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)+\ldots s^{k}\cdot b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)+o(s^{k})\ .

Let
(q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
be the image of the
k
-jet
(b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
under the transformation of adapted coordinates
\Psi^{(k)}(t)
. That is,
for
m=1,2,\ldots,k
we have

q^{(m)}(t,\Delta u):=\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}(t)[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha}]\ .

(3.9)

Then, curves
q^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
satisfy the following system of ODEs:

{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\cdot\left\{\sum_{\alpha,\ \beta,\ w(\alpha)+w(\beta)=m-1}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|+1)}(t)\left[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha}\right]\right\}

(3.10)

Proof.

The fact that the map
\Psi^{(k)}(t)
is well-defined will be proved in the next paragraph (Lemma
3.4
), where we shall give a geometric interpretation of this construction.

Let us address the problem of reversibility. Note that for each
t\in[0,T]
the family
\{\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)\}_{a=1,\ldots,n}
is a basis of
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(t)}M
. Indeed this is easily seen from
(
3.4
)
, which easily implies that

\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)[b^{(1)}(t)]=\operatorname{const}

whenever
b^{(1)}(t)
is subject to

\dot{b}^{(1)}(t)=\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\cdot\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(1)}(t)]
. The latter is the evolution in
\mathrm{T}M
induced by the flow of the (time-dependent)
control vector field
q\mapsto\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(q)
. As this flow consists of diffeomorphisms (see
[
JR16
, Sec. 2]
), then the evolution of
b^{(1)}(t)
is reversible, and hence so is the evolution of
\Phi^{(1)}(t):=\left(\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)\right)_{a=1,2\ldots,n}
.

We conclude that the assignment

\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M\ni b^{(1)}\longmapsto\left(q^{(1)}=\Phi^{(1)}(t)[b^{(1)}]\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

is reversible for each
t\in[0,T]
. Similarly, so is

\mathrm{T}^{2}_{q(t)}M\ni(b^{(1)},b^{(2)})\longmapsto\left(q^{(1)},q^{(2)}=\Phi^{(1)}(t)[b^{(2)}]+\Phi^{(2)}(t)[b^{(1)},b^{(1)}]\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\ ,

as we may express
b^{(1)}
in terms of
q^{(1)}
and then reverse
\Phi^{(1)}(t)
to get
b^{(2)}
from
q^{(2)}
.

In higher degrees an analogous argument proves that formulas (
3.8
) define isomorphisms between the space of
k
-jets of curves in
M
at
q(t)
and the space
\mathbb{R}^{k\cdot n}

\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M\ni(b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})\longmapsto(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})\in\mathbb{R}^{k\cdot n}\ .

Finally note that formulas (
3.8
) respect the gradings given by
\deg(b^{(m)})=\deg(q^{(m)})=m
.

Now let us prove the remaining part of the assertion. Differentiation of (
3.9
) gives us

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha}]+\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot a_{l}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}[\dot{b}^{(l)},\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha-1_{l}}]\ ,

where naturally
\alpha-1_{l}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l-1},a_{l}-1,a_{l+1},\ldots,a_{k})
. Now note that by (
3.7
) the derivatives
{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}_{a}
are linear in
u_{i}
’s, while by (
2.2
) the derivatives
\dot{b}^{(l)}
have a part linear in
u_{i}
’s and a part linear in
\Delta u_{i}
’s. Therefore,
{\dot{q}}^{(m)}
splits into a part linear in
u_{i}
’s and a part linear in
\Delta u_{i}
’s, i.e

{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot A_{i}+\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot B_{i}\ ,

where
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
do not depend on neither
u_{i}
’s, nor
\Delta u_{i}
’s.
We will now calculate these two parts separately, proving that the first one is zero, while the second one is precisely the right-hand side of (
3.10
). This will end the proof.

Let us begin with the second part. Since
{\dot{\Phi}}^{(m)}(t)
do not depend on
\Delta u_{i}
’s, only the derivatives
\dot{b}^{(l)}
contribute giving

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot B_{i}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot a_{l}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}[\text{part of $\dot{b}^{(l)}$ linear in $\Delta u_{i}$'s},\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}]\overset{\eqref{eqn:bb_k}}{=}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot a_{l}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}\left[\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=l-1}\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}]\right\},b(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{(\alpha-1_{l})!}\cdot\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=l-1}\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha-1_{l}|+1)}\left[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}\right]\right\}

Now observe that a triple
(\alpha,l,\beta)
where
w(\alpha)=m
,
a_{l}>0
and
w(\beta)=l-1
uniquely determines a pair of multi-indexes
(\gamma=\alpha-1_{l},\beta)
satisfying
w(\gamma)+w(\beta)=(m-l)+l-1=m-1
. Thus we may change the summation order in the expression above to obtain

\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot B_{i}=\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\gamma,\ \beta,\ w(\gamma)+w(\beta)=m-1}\frac{1}{\gamma!}\cdot\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\gamma|+1)}[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t)^{\gamma}]\right\}

in agreement with (
3.10
).

Now let us calculate the part of
{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
linear in
u_{i}
’s. Proceeding as in the previous part of the proof we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle\cdot A_{i}=\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha}]+\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot a_{l}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}[\text{part of $\dot{b}^{(l)}$ linear in $u_{i}$'s},\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha-1_{l}}]\overset{\eqref{eqn:bb_k}}{=}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha}]+\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot a_{l}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}\left[\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=l}\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}]\right\},b(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha}]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{(\alpha-1_{l})!}\cdot\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=l}\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha-1_{l}|+1)}\left[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}\right]\right\}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha}]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\gamma,\ \beta,\ w(\gamma)+w(\beta)=m}\frac{1}{\gamma!}\cdot\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\gamma|+1)}[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t)^{\gamma}]\right\},

where the changes of summation order in the last passage are made analogously as before.
We would like to show that the above equals to zero. To see this note that formula (
3.7
) describes the evolution of a
m
-linear map
\Phi^{(m)}
. Due to the symmetry of
\Phi^{(m)}
we also have

-{\dot{\Phi}}^{(m)}[v_{1},....,v_{m}]=\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}}\sum_{s=1}^{m}\frac{1}{s!(m-s)!}\Phi^{(m-s+1)}\left[\mathrm{D}^{s}_{q(t)}X_{i}[v_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(s)}],v_{\sigma(s+1)},\ldots,v_{\sigma(m)}\right]\right\}

for every
m
-tuple of vectors
v_{1},v_{2}\ldots,v_{m}\in\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha}]=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}u_{i}\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{|\alpha|}}\sum_{s=1}^{|\alpha|}\frac{1}{s!({|\alpha|}-s)!}\Phi^{({|\alpha|}-s+1)}[\mathrm{D}^{s}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b^{(\sigma(1))},\ldots,b^{(\sigma(s))}],b^{(\sigma(s+1))},\ldots,b^{(\sigma({|\alpha|}))}]

Due to the fact that
\Phi^{({|\alpha|}-s+1)}_{a}
and
\mathrm{D}^{s}_{q(t)}X_{i}
are multi-linear, we may identify two permutations that have the same
s
initial elements. The number of such permutations for a given
\alpha
is precisely
s!(|\alpha|-s)!
. It follows that we can identify the above triple sum over
(\alpha,\sigma,s)
as a sum over sub-divisions of the multi-index
\alpha
into a sum
\alpha=\beta+\gamma
(so
s=|\beta|
,
|\alpha|-s=|\gamma|
, and
w(\alpha)=w(\beta)+w(\gamma)
). The latter are however taken with multiplicity
\binom{\alpha}{\beta}=\prod_{i}\binom{\alpha_{i}}{\beta_{i}}
, as
\binom{\alpha_{i}}{\beta_{i}}
is a number of ways an ordered sequence of
\alpha_{i}
elements
(b_{i},\ldots,b_{i})
can be divided between in two groups of
\beta_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}
elements. Since
\alpha!=\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\cdot\beta!\cdot\gamma!
. We arrive at

\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha}]=-\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\gamma,\ \beta,\ w(\gamma)+w(\beta)=m}\frac{1}{\gamma!}\cdot\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\gamma|+1)}[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\gamma}]\right\}\ ,

proving that
\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot A_{i}=0
. This ends the proof.
∎

Example 3.3

(Generalized Martinet system, part 2.)
.

Let us apply the above theory to Example
2.2
. Equation (
3.4
) reads as

{\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}(t)[\cdot]=-\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{2}[\cdot]\right]=\big\langle\operatorname{d}x_{1},\cdot\big\rangle\cdot\Phi^{(1)}(t)[\partial_{x_{2}}]\ .

For a natural choice of initial value
\Phi^{(1)}(0)=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
this implies that
\Phi^{(1)}(t)
is the following linear isomorphism on
\mathbb{R}^{3}

\Phi^{(1)}(t)[\partial_{x_{1}}]=\partial_{x_{1}}+t\cdot\partial_{x_{2}},\quad\Phi^{(1)}(t)[\partial_{x_{2}}]=\partial_{x_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi^{(1)}(t)[\partial_{x_{3}}]=\partial_{x_{3}}.

In higher degrees
\Phi^{(m)}(t)\equiv 0
, for
m=2,3,\ldots,p-1,p+1,\ldots
, with the sole exception of a single component of
\Phi^{(p)}(t)
, namely

\Phi^{(p)}(t)[\underbrace{\partial_{x_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{x_{1}}}_{p}]=-p!\cdot t\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}\ .

We can now use formula (
3.8
) definining the transformation of adapted coordinates to arrive at

\displaystyle q^{(1)}(t)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(1)}(t)\right]

\displaystyle q^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(2)}(t)\right]+\Phi^{(2)}(t)\left[b^{(1)}(t),b^{(1)}(t)\right]=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(2)}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle q^{(m)}(t)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(m)}(t)\right]

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle q^{(p)}(t)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(p)}(t)\right]+\frac{1}{p!}\Phi^{(p)}(t)\left[b^{(1)}(t)\ldots,b^{(1)}(t)\right]=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(p)}(t)\right]-{t\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}}

\displaystyle q^{(p+1)}(t)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(p+1)}(t)\right]+\frac{1}{(p-1)!}\Phi^{(p)}(t)\left[b^{(1)}(t)\ldots,b^{(1)}(t),b^{(2)}(t)\right]=

\displaystyle\phantom{==}\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(p+1)}(t)\right]-p\cdot t\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p-1}b^{(2)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}

We can differentiate the above formulas, or use evolution equations (
3.10
) to get evolution equations for
q^{(m)}
’s which have a bit simpler form then those for
b^{(m)}
’s

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(1)}(t)

\displaystyle=\Delta u_{1}(t)\cdot(\partial_{x_{1}}+t\cdot\partial_{x_{2}})+\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(m-1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(p)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(p-1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}-\Delta u_{1}(t)\cdot p\cdot t\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p-1}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(p+1)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot b^{(p)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}+\Delta u_{2}(t)\left(b^{(1)}_{1}\right)^{p}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}+\Delta u_{1}(t)\cdot p(p-1)\cdot t\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p-2}b^{(2)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}\ .

The solutions are

\displaystyle q^{(1)}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=b^{(1)}_{1}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{1}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau

\displaystyle q^{(1)}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)+\int_{0}^{t}\tau\cdot\Delta u_{1}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau

\displaystyle q^{(2)}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\cdot b^{(1)}_{1}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau=-\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\Delta u_{1}(s)\operatorname{d}s\right)\operatorname{d}\tau

\displaystyle q^{(p)}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=-t\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p}=-t\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{1}(\tau)\operatorname{d}\tau\right)^{p}

\displaystyle q^{(p+1)}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\left(b^{(1)}_{1}(\tau)\right)^{p}\operatorname{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\Delta u_{2}(\tau)\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\Delta u_{1}(s)\operatorname{d}s\right)^{p}\operatorname{d}\tau\ ,

with all other components
q^{(m)}_{i}(t)
, for
m=1,2,\ldots,p+1
, equal to zero.

Geometric interpretation of the adapted coordinates

So far the construction of adapted coordinates may be seen as a computational trick which helps to simplify the evolution equations for curves
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
. However, as we shall see in this paragraph, it has a natural geometric interpretation.

Consider
q(t)
a trajectory of the control-linear system (

Λ

) corresponding to the control
u\in\Omega
and initial condition
q(0)=q_{0}
.
Let now
q_{s}(t)
be a family of solutions of (

Λ

) corresponding to the same control
u
and a smooth curve of initial conditions
s\mapsto q_{s}(0)
, passing through
q_{0}=q_{0}(0)
(so that
q_{s=0}(t)=q(t)
). It turns out that the assignment
q_{s}(0)\longmapsto q_{s}(t)
gives rise to a well-defined map on
k
-jets at
s=0
:
1
1

1

This is a simple consequence of the theorem about the regularity of the dependence of a solution of an ODE (in the sense of Caratheodory) on the initial condition.

\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q_{0}}M\ni[q_{s}(0)]_{\sim k}\longmapsto[q_{s}(t)]_{\sim k}\in\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M\ .

We shall denote this map – the
k
-th tangent lift of the flow of the control vector field
X_{u}
– by
\mathrm{T}^{k}X^{t}_{u}
. It describes the natural action on
k
-jets of the evolution (flow) of the control system (

Λ

) for a fixed control
u
. The following results explain the relation of
\mathrm{T}^{k}X^{t}_{u}
with the transformation of adapted coordinates.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
q(t)
be a trajectory of the control-linear system (

Λ

) corresponding to a control
u\in\Omega
. Consider a 1-parameter family of
k
-jets
\vec{b}(t)=(b^{(1)}(t),\ldots,b^{(k)}(t))\in\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M
. Then

\vec{b}(t)=\mathrm{T}^{k}X_{u}^{t}[\vec{b}(0)]\quad\text{for every $t\in[0,T]$ if and only if}

\vec{b}(t)=(b^{(1)}(t),\ldots,b^{(k)}(t))
is constant under the transformation of adapted coordinates
\Psi^{(k)}(t)
.

In other words

\left(\Psi^{(k)}(t)\right)^{-1}[(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})]=\vec{b}(t)=\mathrm{T}^{k}X_{u}^{t}[\vec{b}(0)]\ ,

where
(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})=\Psi^{(k)}(0)[\vec{b}(0)]
. In particular, the construction of the transformation of adapted coordinates does not depend on the choice of the local coordinate system on
M
.

Proof.

Choose a local coordinate system on
M
and let
q_{s}(t)
be a family of solutions of (

Λ

) as described at the beginning of this paragraph. Now

\dot{q_{s}}(t)=\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\quad\text{with the initial condition $q_{s}(0)$,}

hence
\vec{b}(t)=(b^{(1)}(t),\ldots,b^{(k)}(t))
– the
k
-jet of
s\mapsto q_{s}(t)
at
s=0
, which by definition equals to
\mathrm{T}^{k}X_{u}^{t}[\vec{b}(0)]
– satisfies an ODE obtained by a repetitive differentiation of the above equation with respect to
s
. This will, however, be a special case of an ODE obtained by an analogous differentiation of equation (
2.1
) if we take
\Delta u=0
. As we know from Lemma
2.1
, the latter equation is just (
2.2
), and so
\vec{b}(t)
is subject to (
2.2
) for
\Delta u=0
. Now, by the results of Theorem
3.2
, after an application of the transformation of adapted coordinates, the curve
\vec{q}(t)=(q^{(1)}(t),\ldots,q^{(k)}(t))=\Psi^{(k)}(t)[\vec{b}(t)]
would satisfy equation (
3.10
) with
\Delta u=0
. Thus
\dot{\vec{q}}(t)=0
which ends the proof.
∎

Getting rid of
b^{(m)}
’s

Our initial motivation behind the construction of adapted coordinates was to simplify the evolution equations for the polynomial expansion of
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\cdot\Delta u]
. As a result, we got the formula (
3.10
). Note, however, that as
q^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
’s and
b^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
’s are related by means of the transformation of adapted coordinates which is invertible, it is possible to express the right-hand side of (
3.10
) in terms of
q^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)
’s only. Thus we can interpret equation (
3.10
) as a time-dependent control-linear system in variables
(q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
(or to view it differently a control-affine system in variables
(t,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
), where
\Delta u\in\Omega
plays the role of a control. This observation is summarised as follows.

Theorem 3.5
.

Consider a trajectory
q(t)
of a control-linear system (

Λ

) corresponding to the control
u\in\Omega
and choose
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there exists an affine distribution
\mathcal{D}^{(k)}=\partial_{t}+\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{Y_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,Y_{l}^{(k)}\}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\ldots\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}_{k}\ni(t,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
with the following properties:

(i)

consider a natural graded space structure on
\mathbb{R}^{1+kn}
by setting
\deg(t)=0
and
\deg(q^{(m)}_{a})=m
. Then for every
i=1,2,\ldots,l
the field
Y_{i}^{(k)}
is a homogeneous vector field of degree
-1

(ii)

for every control
\Delta u\in\mathrm{T}_{u}\Omega\simeq\Omega
the curve
p^{(k)}(t):=(t,q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
– where

(q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
is the image of the
k
-jet of
s\mapsto\mathrm{End}^{t}[u+s\cdot\Delta u]
at
s=0
under the transformation of adapted coordinates
\Psi^{(k)}(t)
considered in Theorem
3.2
– is a trajectory of the control-affine system defined by
\mathcal{D}^{(k)}
for the control
\Delta u
, that is

\dot{p}^{(k)}(t)=\partial_{t}+\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\cdot Y_{i}^{(k)}(p^{(k)}(t))\qquad\text{with $p^{(k)}(0)=(0,0,\ldots,0)$.}

(
\Sigma^{(k)}(\Delta u)
)

Moreover, for each
k=2,3,\ldots
, the field
Y^{(k)}_{i}
projects to
Y^{(k-1)}_{i}
under the mapping

\mathbb{R}^{1+nk}\ni(t,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k-1)},q^{(k)})\longmapsto(t,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k-1)})\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n(k-1)}\ .

Proof.

Fix a natural
k
and consider evolution equations (
3.10
). They are of the form

{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\cdot\widetilde{Y}^{(m)}_{i}(t,\vec{b}(t,\Delta u))\ ,

where
m=1,2,\ldots,k
and
\widetilde{Y}^{(m)}_{i}(t,\vec{b}(t,\Delta u))
is polynomial in
\vec{b}(t,\Delta u):=(b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
of graded degree
m-1
. Now we may substitute
\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)
with
\vec{q}(t,\Delta u):=(q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),\ldots,q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u))
using the inverse of the transformation of adapted coordinates
\Psi^{(k)}(t)
. Note that the latter transformation intertwines the natural graded structures in the space of
b^{(m)}
’s and
q^{(m)}
’s. Thus we get

{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\Delta u)=\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\cdot Y_{i}^{(m)}(t,\vec{q}(t,\Delta u))\ ,

where
Y^{(m)}(t,\vec{q})=\widetilde{Y}^{(m)}_{i}(t,\Psi^{(k)}(t)^{-1}[\vec{q}])
is polynomial in
\vec{q}
of graded degree
m-1
. ∎

Let us see how this result looks in low degrees. Consider the linear isomorphism
\Phi^{(1)}(t):\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
and denote its inverse by
A(t):\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M
.
Then

\displaystyle b^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)=A(t)[q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)]\quad\text{and}\quad b^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)=A(t)[q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u)]-\frac{1}{2!}A(t)\Phi^{(2)}[Aq^{(1)}(t,\Delta u),Aq^{(1)}(t,\Delta u)]\ ,

and hence equations (

⁢

Σ

(
k
)

(

⁢
Δ
u

)

) look as follows

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot\Phi^{(1)}[X_{i}]

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot\left\{\Phi^{(1)}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[Aq^{(1)}]\right]+\Phi^{(2)}\left[X_{i},Aq^{(1)}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}\cdot\left\{\Phi^{(1)}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[Aq^{(2)}]\right]-\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(1)}\left[A\Phi^{(2)}[Aq^{(1)},Aq^{(1)}\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(1)}\left[\mathrm{D}^{2}X_{i}[Aq^{(1)},Aq^{(1)}\right]+\right.

\displaystyle\Phi^{(2)}\left[\mathrm{D}X_{i}[Aq^{(1)}],Aq^{(1)}\right]+\Phi^{(2)}\left[X_{i},Aq^{(2)}\right]-

\displaystyle\left.\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(2)}\left[X_{i},A\Phi^{(2)}[Aq^{(1)},Aq^{(1)}]\right]+\frac{1}{2!}\Phi^{(3)}\left[X_{i},Aq^{(1)},Aq^{(1)}\right]\right\}

Unfortunately, we were unable to derive exact formulas for
{\dot{q}}^{(m)}
’s in every degree, as they become very complicated with the increasing
m
.

Example 3.6

(Generalized Martinet system, part 3.)
.

In the previous Example
3.3
we derived adapted coordinates and their evolution equations for a generalized Martinet system. In that particular situation it is easy to reverse the transformation of adapted coordinates to arrive at

\displaystyle b^{(m)}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=q^{(m)}_{1}\qquad\text{for $m=1,2,\ldots,p,p+1$;}

\displaystyle b^{(m)}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=q^{(m)}_{2}(t)-t\cdot q^{(m)}_{1}(t)\qquad\text{for $m=1,2,\ldots,p,p+1$; and}

\displaystyle b^{(m)}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=q^{(m)}_{3}(t)\qquad\text{for $m=1,2,\ldots,p-1$; and}

\displaystyle b^{(p)}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=q^{(p)}_{3}(t)+t\left(q^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p}

\displaystyle b^{(p+1)}_{3}(t)

\displaystyle=q^{(p+1)}_{3}(t)+t\cdot p\left(q^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p-1}q^{(2)}_{1}(t)

Thus evolution equations become

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(1)}(t)

\displaystyle=\Delta u_{1}(t)\cdot(\partial_{x_{1}}+t\cdot\partial_{x_{2}})+\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot q^{(1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot q^{(m-1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(p)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot q^{(p-1)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}-\Delta u_{1}(t)\cdot p\cdot t\left(q^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p-1}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}

\displaystyle{\dot{q}}^{(p+1)}(t)

\displaystyle=-\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot q^{(p)}_{1}(t)\cdot\partial_{x_{2}}-\Delta u_{1}(t)\cdot p(p-1)\cdot t\left(q^{(1)}_{1}(t)\right)^{p-2}q^{(2)}_{1}\cdot\partial_{x_{3}}+\Delta u_{2}(t)\cdot p\left(q^{(1)}_{1}\right)^{p}\partial_{x_{3}}\ .

Therefore the fields
Y^{(p+1)}_{1}
and
Y^{(p+1)}_{2}
described in Theorem
3.5
are

\displaystyle Y^{(p)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{q^{(1)}_{1}}+t\cdot\partial_{q^{(1)}_{2}}-p\cdot t\left(q^{(1)}_{1}\right)^{p-1}\cdot\partial_{q^{(p)}_{3}}-p(p-1)\cdot t\left(q^{(1)}_{1}\right)^{p-2}q^{(2)}_{1}\cdot\partial_{q^{(p+1)}_{3}}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle Y^{(p)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{q^{(1)}_{2}}-\sum_{m=1}^{p+1}q^{(m-1)}_{1}\cdot\partial_{q^{(m)}_{2}}+p\left(q^{(1)}_{1}\right)^{p}\cdot\partial_{q^{(p+1)}_{3}}\ .

4
Examples and applications

4.1
Adapted coordinates for invariant systems on Lie groups

The geometric setting

Let us consider a special situation of a control-linear system (

Λ

) with
M=G
being a Lie group, and
\mathcal{D}
being a left-invariant distribution on
G
, determined by an
l
-dimensional subspace
\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}\}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{T}_{e}G
. For simplicity of notation, in the calculations we will assume that
G
is a matrix group, and we will denote the matrix multiplication by
\circ
. We shall, however, address a general situation at the end of this subsection.

In the above setting vector fields spanning
\mathcal{D}
are simply
X_{i}(g)=g\circ e_{i}
, and we may naturally identify a control
u(t)=(u_{1}(t),u_{2}(t),\ldots,u_{l}(t))
at
t
with an element
\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)e_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
. Now equation (

Λ

) reads simply as

\dot{g}(t)=g(t)\circ u(t);\qquad g(0)=e,

(4.1)

where for simplicity we set the initial point to be the group identity
e\in G
. Now a simple calculation shows that, for a given control
\Delta u\in\mathrm{T}_{u}\Omega\simeq\Omega
, curves
b^{(m)}(t)
satisfy the following system of ODEs:

\begin{split}\dot{b}^{(1)}(t)&=b^{(1)}(t)\circ u(t)+g(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\\
\dot{b}^{(2)}(t)&=b^{(2)}(t)\circ u(t)+2\cdot b^{(1)}(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\\
&\ldots\\
\dot{b}^{(k)}(t)&=b^{(k)}(t)\circ u(t)+k\cdot b^{(k-1)}(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\end{split}

(4.2)

Calculation of adapted coordinates

Now we would like to apply Theorem
3.2
and construct adapted coordinates in the above setting. The comparison of the above system (
4.2
) with general formulas (
2.2
) reveals that
\mathrm{D}_{g(t)}X_{i}[b]=b\circ e_{i}
, and
\mathrm{D}^{m}_{g(t)}X_{i}\equiv 0
for
m>1
. Hence, by the results of Theorem
3.2
, also
\Phi^{(m)}_{a}(t)\equiv 0
for all
m>1
. On the other hand, covectors
\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)
should satisfy

0={\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}(t)[b]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\mathrm{D}_{g(t)}X_{i}[b]\right]={\dot{\Phi}}^{(1)}_{a}(t)[b]+\Phi^{(1)}_{a}\left[b\circ u(t)\right]\ ,

with
\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(0)=:\xi_{a}
being a fixed basis of
\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{e}G=\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
. Let us identify
\mathfrak{g}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by means of

I:\mathfrak{g}\ni b\longmapsto\left(\big\langle\xi_{1},b\big\rangle,\ldots,\big\langle\xi_{n},b\big\rangle\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ .

Now a simple calculation shows that
\Phi^{(1)}(t)[b]:=I^{-1}\left(\Phi^{(1)}_{1}(t)[b],\ldots,\Phi^{(1)}_{n}(t)[b]\right)
is just

\Phi^{(1)}(t)[b]=b\circ g(t)^{-1}\ .

By (
3.10
), we conclude that for each
m=1,2,3,\ldots
the adapted coordinates
q^{(m)}(t)=\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(m)}(t)\right]=b^{(m)}(t)\circ g(t)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{g}
satisfy

{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t)=m\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[\mathrm{D}_{g(t)}X_{i}\left[b^{(m-1)}(t)\right]\right]=m\ \Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[b^{(m-1)}(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\right]=m\ b^{(m-1)}(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\circ g(t)^{-1}.

In this particular situation, we may easily get rid of
b^{(m)}
’s to arrive at the following system of ODEs:

\begin{split}{\dot{q}}^{(1)}(t)&=g(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\circ g(t)^{-1}=\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t)}\Delta u(t)\\
{\dot{q}}^{(2)}(t)&=2\ b^{(1)}(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\circ g(t)^{-1}=2\ q^{(1)}(t)\circ g(t)\circ\Delta u(t)\circ g(t)^{-1}=2\ q^{(1)}(t)\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t)}\Delta u(t)\\
&\ldots\\
{\dot{q}}^{(k)}(t)&=k\ q^{(k-1)}(t)\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t)}\Delta u(t)\ .\end{split}

(4.3)

This leads to the following set of solutions

\begin{split}b^{(1)}(t)&=\left[\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t_{1})}\Delta u(t_{1})\operatorname{d}t_{1}\right]\circ g(t)\\
b^{(2)}(t)&=2\left[\int_{0}^{t}q^{(1)}(t_{1})\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t_{1})}\Delta u(t_{1})\operatorname{d}t_{1}\right]\circ g(t)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad=2\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t_{2})}\Delta u(t_{2})\operatorname{d}t_{2}\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t_{1})}\Delta u(t_{1})\operatorname{d}t_{1}\right]\circ g(t)\\
&\ldots\\
b^{(k)}(t)&=k!\left[\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\ldots\int_{0}^{t_{k-1}}\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t_{k})}\Delta u(t_{k})\circ\ldots\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t_{1})}\Delta u(t_{1})\operatorname{d}t_{k}\ldots\operatorname{d}t_{1}\right]\circ g(t),\end{split}

(4.4)

which are in perfect agreement with the results of
[
LD21
]
, where the same formulas were obtained by a different method.

Remark about general Lie groups

Finally, we would like to comment about the geometric sense of the formulas (
4.4
) on a general Lie group, where, at the level of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, there is no obvious analogue of the matrix multiplication
\circ
. To do this observe that our basic equation (
4.1
) reads as

\dot{g}(t)=F(g(t),u(t))\ ,

(4.5)

where
F:G\times\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathrm{T}G
is the left trivialization of
\mathrm{T}G
, i.e.
F(g,a)=\mathrm{T}L_{g}[a]
, with
L_{g}:G\rightarrow G
given by
L_{g}(h)=gh
. For our purposes, it will be convenient to treat
F
as a restriction of the map
\mathrm{T}m:\mathrm{T}G\times\mathrm{T}G\rightarrow\mathrm{T}G
– the tangent map to the group multiplication
m:G\times G\rightarrow G
– to
G\times\mathrm{T}_{e}G\subset\mathrm{T}G\times\mathrm{T}G
, where on the first leg we embed
G
into
\mathrm{T}G
as the zero section. Now system (
4.2
) describes the
k
-jet of (
4.5
), i.e. it is constructed by feeding
\mathrm{T}^{k}F:\mathrm{T}^{k}G\times\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G
with the
k
-th jet
(g,b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})\in\mathrm{T}^{k}_{g}G
, and
(u,\Delta u,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}\equiv\underbrace{\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\ldots\times\mathfrak{g}}_{k+1}
, and composing the result with the canonical flip
\kappa:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G\simeq\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}G
to get a vector tangent to
\mathrm{T}^{k}G
. Remembering that
F
is a restriction of
\mathrm{T}m
, we conclude that, up to the canonical identification
\kappa:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G\simeq\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}^{k}G
, the multiplication in the system (
4.2
) is the multiplication
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}m:\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G\times\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G\rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G
applied to a particular pair of
k
-jets embedded in
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G
. At this point, it is worth to remark, that
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G
with the multiplication
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}m
is actually a Lie group – see
[
KMS93
, 37.16]
.

Finally, in the setting of Lie groups we pass to adapted coordinates by the right translation by
g(t)^{-1}
acting on the
k
-jet
(q,b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})
, i.e

(e,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})=\mathrm{T}^{k}R_{g(t)^{-1}}(g,b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})

Now since in the multiplication we have
m(R_{g}f,h)=m(fg,h)=m(f,gh)=m(f,L_{g}h)
, setting
(g,b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(k)})=\mathrm{T}^{k}R_{g(t)}(e,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
in (
4.2
) does not changes the multiplication
\mathrm{T}^{k}F\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}m
, but translates to applying
\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t)}
to the element
\Delta u(t)
and vanishing the
u(t)
-part because of the evolution equations.

Summing up, the multiplication on the right-hand side of the system (
4.3
) is the multiplication
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}m
of an element
(e,q^{(1)},\ldots,q^{(k)})\in\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G
by an element
(0,\operatorname{Ad}_{g(t)}\Delta u(t),0,\ldots,0)\in\mathfrak{g}^{k+1}\equiv\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathfrak{g}\subset\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{T}G
. This, however, does not mean that we have a multiplication between elements of
\mathfrak{g}
. In general, there is no canonical identification of
\mathrm{T}^{k}_{e}G
with
\mathfrak{g}^{k}
(such an identification requires a choice of local coordinates – see Remark
1.1
– or, as happens with matrix groups, an embedding of
G
into
M_{N}(\mathbb{R})
). Thus the resulting formulas (
4.4
) have only a local sense.

4.2
Optimality conditions in sub-Riemannian geometry

In Sec.
3
we were able to associate a control-affine system (

⁢

Σ

(
k
)

(

⁢
Δ
u

)

) with the problem of calculating the derivatives (or, more precisely, the jet-expansion) of the end-point map at a given trajectory of a control-linear system (

Λ

). The results of Sussmann and Jurdjevic
[
SJ72
]
give simple criteria for local non-controllability of a control system at a given point. It turns out that for
k=1
and
k=2
these criteria are closely related to necessary optimality conditions in sub-Riemannian geometry. Below we shall explain this relation.

Sub-Riemannian geodesic problem

Consider the control-linear system (

Λ

) with
\Omega=L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
. For a trajectory
q(t)
, which corresponds to a control
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
, we define its
energy
at
t
as

E^{t}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{i}u_{i}(\tau)^{2}\operatorname{d}\tau\ .

Given
T>0
, and a pair of points
q_{0},q_{1}\in M
, the question is to find a trajectory of (

Λ

) which joins
q_{0}=q(0)
with
q_{1}=q(T)
while minimizing the energy
E^{T}
. Solutions of such a problem are called
minimizing sub-Riemannian geodesics
– see
[
Mon06
,
Rif14
]
for more details.

There is a fundamental relation between optimality in the above sense, and the properties of the
extended end-point map

\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{t}:\Omega\longrightarrow M\times\mathbb{R}
; defined by
\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{t}(u):=(\mathrm{End}^{t}(u),E^{t}(u))
.

Fact 4.1
.

If
\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
is open at
u
, then the corresponding trajectory cannot be a sub-Riemannian geodesics.

All the known optimality criteria for sub-Riemannian geodesics, with the sole exception of
[
HLD16
]
, depend on the above result. They use some version of a (higher-order) open mapping theorem (see
[
Sus03
]
for a set of general results of this type) linking the properties of (first or higher) derivatives of the end-point map
\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
with the openness of
\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
itself. In what follows we shall revise some of these classical results using the construction of adapted coordinates from Theorems
3.2
and
3.5
.

Sub-Riemannian optimality conditions of degree one

It follows easily from the standard open mapping theorem that if
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
(which is of course a vector space) equals
\mathrm{T}_{(q(T),E^{T}(u))}(M\times\mathbb{R})
, then
\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
is open at
u
, and hence the related trajectory
q(t)
cannot be optimal. Thus an obvious necessary condition for optimality is that
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
should be a proper vector subspace in the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{(q(T),E^{T}(u))}(M\times\mathbb{R})
. Curves satisfying this condition are called
extremals
. Among all extremals we distinguish a subclass of
abnormal trajectories
.

Definition 4.2
.

A trajectory
q(t)
of (

Λ

) corresponding to a control
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
is called
abnormal
if
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}
is a proper vector subspace of the tangent space
\mathrm{T}_{q(T)}M
. In particular, an abnormal trajectory is an extremal.

Below we will concentrate solely on this class of curves as they are the most interesting (and mysterious) type of extremals
[
Mon06
]
. The basic reason for this is that an abnormal trajectory is an extremal regardless of the properties of the energy functional, thus being abnormal is a geometric property of the control system itself.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
q(t)
, with
t\in[0,T]
, be an abnormal SR trajectory corresponding to the control
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
. Then the related control-affine system
(\Sigma^{(1)})
in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(t,q^{(1)})
described in Thm
3.5
is not controllable at any point
(t,0)
. In particular, there exist an
n
-tuple
(\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
such that the covector
\phi(t)=\sum_{a}\phi^{a}\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)\in\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(t)}M
satisfies the following condition

\big\langle\phi(t),X_{i}(q(t))\big\rangle=0\quad\text{for all $i=1,2,\ldots,l$ and all $t\in[0,T]$}.

Observe that due to (
3.4
), the covector
\phi(t)
described above is a
Pontryagin covector
, i.e its evolution is compatible with the evolution of (

Λ

) corresponding to
u
:

\big\langle\dot{\phi}(t),b\big\rangle+\big\langle\phi(t),\sum_{i}u_{i}(t)\cdot\mathrm{D}_{q(t)}X_{i}[b]\big\rangle=0\quad\text{ for every $b\in\mathrm{T}_{q(t)}M$.}

Proof.

By definition, since
q(t)
is an abnormal trajectory, we know that
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}=\{b^{(1)}(T,\Delta u)\ |\ \Delta u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})\}
is a proper vector subspace of
\mathrm{T}_{q(T)}M
. Passing to the adapted coordinates
q^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
– which in degree one involves a linear transformation – we conclude that the space
V:=\{q^{(1)}(T,\Delta u)\ |\ \Delta u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})\}
is a proper vector subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. However, by the results of Thm
3.5
, space
V
is just
\mathcal{R}_{T}
– the reachable set at time
T
of the control system

{\dot{q}}^{(1)}(t)=\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\cdot Y^{(1)}_{i}(t)=\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}(t)\cdot\Phi^{(1)}(t)[X_{i}(q(t))];\qquad q^{(1)}(0)=0.

Clearly, by extending a control
\Delta u\in L^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{l})
by zero on
[\tau,T]
, also
\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\subset\mathcal{R}_{T}=V
for every
\tau\in[0,T]
. We conclude that the fields
Y^{(1)}_{i}(t)
must be tangent to
V
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Hence, each non-zero covector
\phi=(\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{n})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
annihilating
V
, will satisfy

0=\big\langle\phi,Y^{(1)}_{i}(t)\big\rangle=\big\langle\phi,\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[X_{i}(q(t))\right]\big\rangle
for every
i=1,2,\ldots,l
and every
t\in[0,T]
. This ends the proof.
∎

Optimality conditions of degree two

A basis of our considerations in this part is the following result

Theorem 4.4

(Agrachew-Sarychew Index Lemma
[
AS96
]
)
.

Let
q(t)
, with
t\in[0,T]
be an abnormal minimizing sub-Riemannian geodesics corresponding to the control
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
, and denote by
r
the codimension of
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}
in
\mathrm{T}_{q(T)}M
. Then there exists a covector
\phi_{0}\in\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(T)}M
with the following properties

•

\phi_{0}
annihilates the image
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}

•

the negative index of the quadratic map

\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}\big\rangle\big|_{\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}}:\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

is at most
r-1
.

Above we treat a covector
\phi_{0}\in\mathrm{T}^{\ast}M
, as an element
\phi_{0}+0\cdot\operatorname{d}r\in\mathrm{T}^{\ast}(M\times\mathbb{R})
– hence its action on
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}
-vectors is trivial.

The above result is stated in terms of the extended end-point map
\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
. It is however easy to reformulate it in the language of the standard end-point map
\mathrm{End}^{T}
for a moderate price of rising the index by one.

Proposition 4.5

(modification of the Agrachew-Sarychew Index Lemma)
.

Let
q(t)
, with
t\in[0,T]
, be an abnormal minimizing sub-Riemannian geodesics corresponding to the control
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
, and denote by
r
the codimension of
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}
in
\mathrm{T}_{q(T)}M
. Then there exists a covector
\phi_{0}\in\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(T)}M
with the following properties

•

\phi_{0}
annihilates the image
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}

•

the negative index of the quadratic map

\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\big\rangle\big|_{\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}}:\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

is at most
r
.

Proof.

Let
\phi_{0}
be as in the assertion of Theorem
4.4
. Since
\phi_{0}
annihilates the
\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}
-direction, then clearly

\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}\big\rangle=\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\big\rangle\ .

Now as
\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}=\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\cap\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}E^{T}
and
E^{T}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-valued map, the space
\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
is a subspace of
\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}
of codimension less or equal 1. Thus if
\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\big\rangle
would be negatively defined on some
r+1
dimensional vector subspace
W\subset\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}
, then
W^{\prime}=W\cap\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}E^{t}\subset\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}
would be a vector subspace of dimension at least
r
on which
\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\big\rangle=\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\bm{\mathrm{End}}^{T}\big\rangle
is negatively defined. This contradicts the assertion of Theorem
4.4
.
∎

Under a further assumption that the index mentioned in Prop.
4.5
is actually zero, results of Theorem
3.5
allow to derive the following
Goh conditions
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
q(t)
