be a sub-Riemannian trajectory corresponding to a control
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
. Assume that a covector
\phi_{0}\in\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(T)}M
satisfies the conditions

(i)

\phi_{0}
annihilates the image
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}

(ii)

the negative index of the quadratic map

\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\big\rangle\big|_{\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}}:\ker\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

is zero.

Then there exists an
n
-tuple
(\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
such that the Pontryagin covector
\phi(t)=\sum_{a}\phi^{a}\Phi^{(1)}_{a}(t)\in\mathrm{T}^{\ast}_{q(t)}M
satisfies the conditions

\big\langle\phi(t),X_{i}(q(t))\big\rangle=0\quad\text{and}\quad\big\langle\phi(t),[X_{i},X_{j}](q(t))\big\rangle=0\qquad\text{for all $i,j=1,2,\ldots,l$ and all $t\in[0,T]$.}

Proof.

By assumption, for every controls
\Delta u^{(1)},\Delta u^{(2)}\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
such that
\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\left[\Delta u^{(1)}\right]=b^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})=0
we have

\big\langle\phi_{0},\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\left[\Delta u^{(1)}\right]=b^{(2)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})\big\rangle\geq 0\quad\text{and}\qquad\big\langle\phi_{0},\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}\left[\Delta u^{(2)}\right]=b^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(2)})\big\rangle=0\ .

Now observe that, since
b^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})=0
, when passing to adapted coordinates formulas (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) give us

q^{(2)}(T,\Delta u_{1})=\Phi^{(1)}(T)\left[b^{(2)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})\right]\quad\text{and}\quad q^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})=\Phi^{(1)}(T)\left[b^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(2)})\right]\ .

Hence for a non-zero covector
\phi_{0}^{\prime}=\Phi^{(1)}(T)^{\ast}\phi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\big\langle\phi_{0}^{\prime},q^{(2)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})\big\rangle\geq 0
and
\big\langle\phi_{0}^{\prime},q^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(2)})\big\rangle=0
, and thus

\big\langle\phi_{0}^{\prime},q^{(2)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})+q^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(2)})\big\rangle\geq 0\qquad\text{whenever $q^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})=\Phi^{(1)}(T)\left[b^{(1)}(T,\Delta u^{(1)})\right]=0$}.

In light of Theorem
3.5
this translates as a condition that the control system

\begin{split}\dot{x}^{(1)}&=\sum_{i}\Delta u^{(1)}_{i}(t)\cdot Y^{(1)}_{i}(t)\\
\dot{x}^{(2)}&=\sum_{i}\Delta u^{(1)}_{i}(t)\cdot Y^{(2)}_{i}(t,x^{(1)})+\sum_{i}\Delta u^{(2)}_{i}(t)\cdot Y^{(1)}_{i}(t)\ ;\end{split}

(4.6)

i.e.
{x}^{(1)}(t)=q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)})
and
{x}^{(2)}(t)=q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)})+q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u^{(2)})
; is not controllable (as a system in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, but also in
V\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
V:=\{q^{(1)}(T,\Delta u)\ |\ \Delta u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})\}
is the reachable set of coordinates
x^{(1)}
– as in the proof of Lemma
4.3
) at time
T
at the point
(x^{(1)}=0,x^{(2)}=0)
. In particular, by the criteria of local controllability of Jurdjevic-Sussmann
[
SJ72
]
, the control fields

Z^{(2)}_{i}(t,x^{(1)}):=(Y^{(1)}_{i}(t),Y^{(2)}_{i}(t,x^{(1)}))\quad\text{and}\quad Z^{(1)}_{i}:=(0,Y^{(1)}_{i}(t))

and their Lie brackets calculated at
(T,x^{(1)}=0,x^{(2)}=0)
are contained in a proper linear subspace
W\subset V\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note also, that the reachable set of the discussed control system at any time
T^{\prime}\in[0,T]
at the point
(x^{(1)}=0,x^{(2)}=0)
is naturally contained in the analogous reachable set at
T
(by simply extending the controls by zero on
[T^{\prime},T]
). Thus we may assume that
W
is spanned by the control vector fields and their brackets calculated at
(T^{\prime},x^{(1)}=0,x^{(2)}=0)
for all
T^{\prime}\in[0,T]
. Further, as the system
\dot{x}^{(1)}=\sum_{i}\Delta u^{(1)}_{i}\cdot Y^{(1)}_{i}(t)
is controllable in
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we may assume that
W=V\times V^{\prime}
, where
V^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a proper vector subspace.

Finally, a simple calculation shows that at time
t

[Z^{(2)}_{i},Z^{(2)}_{j}]=(0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)\Big[[X_{i},X_{j}](q(t))\Big])\ ,

hence, in particular, for every
t\in[0,T]
fields
(0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)\big[[X_{i},X_{j}](q(t))\big])
and
(0,Y^{(1)}_{i}(t))=(0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)\big[X_{i}(q(t))\big])
belong to
V\times V^{\prime}
. Taking
(\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
to be any covector annihilating
V^{\prime}
we get the assertion.
∎

Finally, we may refer to the recent results of
[
Jóź23
]
where it is proved that it is possible to put the Agrachew-Sarychew index to zero by dividing the curve into a finite number of pieces.

Lemma 4.7

(
[
Jóź23
]
)
.

Let
q(t)
, with
t\in[0,T]
be an abnormal minimizing sub-Riemannian geodesics corresponding to the control
u(t)
, and denote by
r
the codimension of
\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{D}_{u}\mathrm{End}^{T}
in
\mathrm{T}_{q(T)}M
. Then there exists at most
r
points
0\leq\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\ldots<\tau_{s}\leq T
such that on every subinterval
[a,b]\subset(\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1})
for
i=1,2,\ldots,s-1
the trajectory
q(t)
with
t\in[a,b]
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.6
.

In particular, on each of the pieces the assertion of Lemma
4.6
holds. Thus we get
Goh conditions
on each piece (perhaps for different Pontryagin covectors).

Actually, the tool of adapted coordinates can be used to give a more refined picture of the second-order optimality conditions in sub-Riemannian geometry. We refer to
[
Jóź23
]
for more details.

5
General polynomial variations of the end-point map

Motivations

Curves
b^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)
(and their counterparts
q^{(k)}(t,\Delta u)
in adapted coordinates) are obtained by studying reactions of the end-point map
\mathrm{End}^{t}
to changes of the control argument of the form
s\mapsto u+s\cdot\Delta u
. It is, however, most natural to study such reactions for a more general family of controls, i.e.

s\longmapsto u+s\cdot\Delta u^{(1)}+s^{2}\cdot\Delta u^{(2)}+\ldots+s^{k}\cdot\Delta u^{(k)}\ .

(5.1)

Geometrically this corresponds to studying the natural lift of the end-point map to the space of
k
-jets, i.e.
\mathrm{T}^{k}\mathrm{End}^{t}:\mathrm{T}^{k}_{u}\Omega\longrightarrow\mathrm{T}^{k}_{q(t)}M
.

Let us observe that such a generalization looks promising from the point of view of applications. First of all, recall formula (
1.1
), which allowed us to understand Agrachev’s approach to the study of second derivatives. Further note that in our interpretation of sub-Riemannian optimality conditions of degree two in Lemma
4.6
, it turned out that the non-controllability of the system (
4.6
) played a crucial role. This system is clearly not constructed from a variation of the end-point map related with the control shift
s\mapsto u+s\cdot\Delta u
, as it involves two controls
\Delta u^{(1)},\Delta u^{(2)}
instead of a single control
\Delta u
. However, it is not difficult to guess that it corresponds to variation by the family of controls

s\mapsto u+s\cdot\Delta u^{(1)}+s^{2}\cdot\Delta u^{(2)}
undergoing a transformation of adapted coordinates.

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}})

are subject to the following system of ODEs:

b^{(m)}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern w(\alpha)=m-r}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\alpha|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t,\Delta u)^{\alpha}]\right)\ ,

(5.2)

where we use the notation introduced on page
1.2
and we denote
\Delta u^{(0)}=u
.

Proof.

It is enough to repeat the proof of Lemma
2.1
. Let
q_{s}(t)
be a solution of

\dot{q_{s}}(t)=\sum_{i}\left(u_{i}(t)+s\cdot\Delta u^{(1)}_{i}(t)+\ldots+s^{k}\cdot\Delta u^{(k)}_{i}(t)\right)X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\ .

Then analogously as before

\partial_{s}^{m}\Big|_{0}\left[X_{i}(q_{s}(t))\right]=m!\cdot\left(\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\alpha|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , while for

This ends the proof.
∎

{\dot{q}}^{(m)}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}})=\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot A_{i}+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{i}\Delta u^{(r)}_{i}\cdot B_{i}^{(r)}\ ,

Like in the proof of Theorem
3.2

\displaystyle\sum_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle\cdot A_{i}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=j}{\dot{\Phi}}^{(|\alpha|)}[\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha}]+\sum_{i}u_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\gamma,\ \beta,\ w(\gamma)+w(\beta)=j}\frac{1}{\gamma!}\cdot\frac{1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\gamma|+1)}[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t)^{\gamma}]\right\}=0\ .

On the other hand

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}^{(r)}\cdot B_{i}^{(r)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot a_{l}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}[\text{part of $\dot{b}^{(l)}$ linear in $\Delta u_{i}^{(r)}$'s},\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\cdot a_{l}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha|)}\left[\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}^{(r)}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=l-r}\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\beta!}\cdot\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}]\right\},b(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Delta u^{(r)}_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\alpha,\ w(\alpha)=m}\sum_{l\leq m}\frac{1}{(\alpha-1_{l})!}\cdot\sum_{\beta,\ w(\beta)=l-r}\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\alpha-1_{l}|+1)}\left[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t)^{\alpha-1_{l}}\right]\right\}

Now observe that a triple
(\alpha,l,\beta)
where
w(\alpha)=j
,
a_{l}>0
and
w(\beta)=l-r
uniquely determines a pair of multi-indexes
(\gamma=\alpha-1_{l},\beta)
satisfying
w(\gamma)+w(\beta)=(m-l)+l-r=m-r
. Thus we may change the summation order in the expression above to obtain

\sum_{i}\Delta u_{i}^{(r)}\cdot B_{i}^{(r)}=\sum_{i}\Delta u^{(r)}_{i}\cdot\left\{\sum_{\gamma,\ \beta,\ w(\gamma)+w(\beta)=m-r}\frac{1}{\gamma!}\cdot\frac{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}{\beta!}\cdot\Phi^{(|\gamma|+1)}[\mathrm{D}^{|\beta|}_{q(t)}X_{i}[\vec{b}(t)^{\beta}],\vec{b}(t)^{\gamma}]\right\}\ .

This ends the proof. ∎

Example

Consider 3-variation in the direction of
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}=(\Delta u^{(1)},\Delta u^{(2)},\Delta u^{(3)})
. In that situation, it is quite easy to express curves
q^{(m)}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern for

in terms of curves

. It turns out that

\displaystyle q^{(1)}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \displaystyle=q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)})

\displaystyle q^{(2)}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta u\hfil$\crcr}}})

\displaystyle=q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)})+q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u^{(2)})

\displaystyle q^{(3)}(t,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern \displaystyle=q^{(3)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)})+2q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)},\Delta u^{(2)})+q^{(1)}(t,\Delta u^{(3)})\ .

Here symbol
q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)},\Delta u^{(2)})
denotes the symmetrization

2q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)},\Delta u^{(2)}):=q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)}+\Delta u^{(2)})-q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(1)})-q^{(2)}(t,\Delta u^{(2)})\ .

A hypothesis related with Goh-conditions

By the results of
Theorem
5.3
system (
5.5
) is a control system in
\mathbb{R}\times\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\ldots\mathbb{R}^{n}}_{k}\ni(t,q^{(1)},q^{(2)},\ldots,q^{(k)})
controlled by a family of vector fields

\displaystyle Z^{(k)}_{i}=

\displaystyle(Y_{i}^{(1)}(t),Y_{i}^{(2)}(t,p^{(1)}),\ldots,Y_{i}^{(k)}(t,p^{(1)},\ldots,p^{(k-1)})),

\displaystyle Z^{(k-1)}_{i}=

\displaystyle(0,Y_{i}^{(1)}(t),\ldots,Y_{i}^{(k-1)}(t,p^{(1)},\ldots,p^{(k-2)})),

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle Z^{(2)}_{i}=

\displaystyle(0,\ldots,0,Y_{i}^{(1)}(t),Y^{(2)}_{i}(t,p^{(1)})),

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{i}=

\displaystyle(0,\ldots,0,Y_{i}^{(1)}(t))

for
m=1,2,\ldots,k
. Based on the proof of Lemma
4.6
we state the following

Hypothesis 5.4
.

Within the setting of Subsection
4.2
, consider a sub-Riemannian abnormal extremal corresponding to a control
u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{l})
. If the assumptions of
[
BMS22
, Thm 1.1]
are satisfied then system (
5.5
) is not-controllable at zero.

If the above hyphothesis would be true, we could get higher-order Goh conditions from Sussmann’s-Jurdvevic’s non-controllability conditions in essentially the same way as was done in the proof of Lemma
4.6
. (Perhaps some other conditions could also be computable following the lines of
[
Agr23
]
.) To back this up, after quite heavy calculations, it is possible to check that for all possible indices
i,j,s\in\{1,2,\ldots,l\}
we have

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{i}=

\displaystyle(0,0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)[X_{i}]),

\displaystyle[Z^{(2)}_{i},Z^{(2)}_{j}]=

\displaystyle(0,0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)[X_{i},X_{j}])\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle[Z^{(3)}_{i},[Z^{(3)}_{j},Z^{(3)}_{s}]]=

\displaystyle(0,0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[[X_{i},[X_{j},X_{s}]]\right])+

\displaystyle(0,0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[A(t)\Phi^{(2)}(t)[X_{i},X_{j}-X_{s}\right])+

\displaystyle(0,0,\Phi^{(1)}(t)\left[\mathrm{D}X_{s}[A(t)\Phi^{(2)}(t)[X_{i},X_{j}]]-\mathrm{D}X_{j}[A(t)\Phi^{(2)}(t)[X_{i},X_{s}]]\right]),

where
A(t)
denotes the inverse of the map
\Phi^{(1)}(t)
. Hence the existence of the covector
(0,0,\psi_{0})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ast}
annihilating all these vector fields will imply the existence of a Pontryagin covector (cf. Lemmta
4.3
and
4.6
)
\phi(t):=\Phi^{(1)}(t)^{\ast}\psi_{0}
such that
\big\langle\phi(t),X_{i}\big\rangle
and
\big\langle\phi(t),[X_{i},X_{j}]\big\rangle
vanish. To get conditions
\big\langle\phi(t),[X_{i},[X_{j},X_{k}]]\big\rangle=0
, we should additionally assume that
\psi_{0}
annihilates the remaining two terms in the triple Lie bracket. We believe that this condition will follow from the assumptions of
[
BMS22
, Thm 1.1]
, i.e. the extremal being of corank 1 and vanishing of the second (intrinsic) derivative of the end-point map at
u
. We shall study this topic in more detail in a future publication.
